What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / computer_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"I came in because I keep losing chunks of time re-reading emails and texts, reopening folders, and mentally replaying conversations before I leave the house or start work, like I’m trying to prove to myself I didn’t make some serious mistake.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, doctor, it's... it's really starting to take a toll on my day-to-day life, and I just don't know how to snap out of it. I came in because I keep losing these massive chunks of time, and it's incredibly frustrating. I work with computers—I've been doing IT and systems work ever since I got my bachelor's—so I'm at a screen all day, and lately, I just can't seem to actually start my work. I'll sit down at my desk, and I find myself endlessly re-reading my emails and texts. I'll read an email I sent the day before, scrutinizing every single word, and then I'll open a folder on my desktop, look at the files, close it, and then I just have to reopen it. I'll open and close the same directory over and over again. 

And it’s not even just when I'm at the computer, either. It happens before I even leave the house in the morning. I'll be standing right by the front door, keys in my hand, ready to go, and I'll just freeze up because I start mentally replaying conversations I had from the day before. I'll play them over and over in my head, dissecting every sentence, trying to remember exactly what I said and how the other person reacted. I just get stuck there in the hallway. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself that I didn’t make some serious mistake. I get this horrible nagging feeling that I said something awful, or that I accidentally deleted a crucial file, or sent the wrong attachment to a client, and I just have to keep checking my outbox or my memory to prove that I didn't ruin everything. By the time I finally convince myself it's okay and actually leave the house or start my tasks, an hour or two has just vanished, and I'm already exhausted before the day has even really begun. I just... I can't seem to let things be without checking them a dozen times.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, absolutely, all the time. It's... it's exactly like that. I'll be sitting there, right in the middle of trying to work through a complicated system design, or maybe I'm trying to prepare a lesson or a tutorial for some of the new hires, and I really need to concentrate. But then, out of nowhere, this thought just barges right in. 

It doesn't even have to do with work most of the time! It’ll be this sudden, intense fear that I might have sent the wrong message to my ex-wife—something completely inappropriate or that could be taken the wrong way. Or I'll get it in my head that I somehow mishandled some old family photos I was backing up, like I accidentally wiped the drive and lost them forever. Or sometimes it's this heavy, sinking feeling that I've let someone in the community down, like I made some careless mistake that really hurt somebody. 

And the frustrating part is, I know I've already been over it. I tell myself, "You already thought about this yesterday, it's fine, you didn't do anything wrong, just get back to work." I try so, so hard to just push it away and ignore it. I'll stare at my monitor and try to force my brain back to the system design, but I just... I can't seem to let it pass. It just sits there, nagging at me, completely hijacking my focus, even though I know, logically, that I've already dealt with it. I'll try to suppress it, but the harder I try to ignore it, the louder it gets, until I just have to stop what I'm doing. It's just exhausting having to fight my own brain like that, you know? It just won't let up.

I'll stare at my monitor and try to force my brain back to the system design, but I just... I can't seem to let it pass. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, exactly, that's... that's really what I was getting at when I first came in and was telling you about losing all those massive chunks of time. It's exactly that. When those thoughts hit me, I can't just sit there and feel anxious about it, I actually feel like I have to do something to fix it, or at least try to prove to myself that the thought isn't true. 

So, like I mentioned earlier with the emails and the texts—if my brain says, "Hey, you sent the wrong attachment to that client," I can't just brush it off. I have to go into my email client, go to the sent folder, find the exact email, and open it. And I'll read it. Every single word. I'll look at the file name of the attachment. And I'll say to myself, "Okay, see? It's the right file." And I'll close it. 

But then, maybe ten seconds later, the thought is back, and it's whispering, "Are you sure? Maybe you misread the file extension. Maybe you missed a typo." So I have to open the folder again. I'll open and close the same directory on my desktop over and over and over, checking the files, closing it, opening it back up. It’s like I’m trapped in this loop. And it's not a quick thing, either. I'll spend forty-five minutes just clicking the same folder, reading the same text message, trying to get this... this sense of certainty that just never seems to stick. 

And it's the exact same process with the mental stuff I was talking about, you know, when I'm stuck by the front door before I leave for work. I'll be holding my keys, ready to walk out, and a thought about a conversation from yesterday pops up. I have to stand right there and mentally replay the entire interaction from start to finish. If I get distracted halfway through the memory, or if I can't remember the exact tone of voice the other person used, I feel this intense pressure to start the whole replay over from the very beginning. I'm just standing there in the hallway, completely frozen, going through this mental script again and again, just desperately trying to prove to myself I didn't make some serious mistake or offend someone. I literally cannot turn the doorknob and leave the house, or sit down at my desk and actually start my IT work, until I've done these routines. That's why the time just vanishes. I just... I have to do it, even though I know how completely irrational it is while I'm doing it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah... yeah, it really has. I mean, it’s hard to hide it when you’re losing that much time, you know? At work, especially with my coworkers, it’s becoming a real problem. I’ve always prided myself on being the reliable one—the guy who gets the system specs done on time, the one people can count on. But because my work has slowed down so much with all this... this checking and re-reading and getting stuck in my head, I’ve actually missed a couple of major design milestones recently. The project managers are starting to ask questions, and I don't know what to tell them. I can't exactly say, "Sorry the architecture document is late, I spent three hours opening and closing a desktop folder." 

And it’s not just the technical deliverables, either. I usually handle a lot of the mentoring for the new hires. I really enjoy doing it, sitting down with them and walking them through our legacy systems. But lately, I’ve had to postpone those mentoring sessions constantly. I'll look at the clock, realize I've lost an hour checking my outbox, and I have to message them and push the meeting back. I used to handle all of that so reliably, and now I'm just constantly making excuses to these young kids who are looking to me for guidance.

And then... well, it’s bleeding into my personal life and community stuff, too, which really hurts. There’s this youth coding workshop that I volunteer for. It really, really matters to me—giving back to the kids, showing them the ropes, getting them excited about computers. But the other weekend, I got stuck at my front door doing that exact mental replay thing I was telling you about earlier. I was just standing there with my keys, agonizing over some passing comment I made to the coordinator the day before, just playing it over and over. By the time I finally convinced myself I hadn't offended him and managed to actually leave the house, I was incredibly late. I walked in totally flustered, completely unprepared for the lesson, and the other volunteers had had to stall for me. I was just... I was so profoundly embarrassed. I felt like I let everyone down—the kids, my friends running the program... It’s just making me feel so awful because I can't really explain to any of them why I'm suddenly acting so flaky and undependable.

Or I'll get it in my head that I somehow mishandled some old family photos I was backing up, like I accidentally wiped the drive and lost them forever. Or sometimes it's this heavy, sinking feeling that I've let someone in the community down, like I made some careless mistake that really hurt somebody. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is, I know I've already been over it. I tell myself, \"You already thought about this yesterday, it's fine, you didn't do anything wrong, just get back to work.\" I try so, so hard to just push it away and ignore it. I'll stare at my monitor and try to force my brain back to the system design, but I just... I can't seem to let it pass. It just sits there, nagging at me, completely hijacking my focus, even though I know, logically, that I've already dealt with it. I'll try to suppress it, but the harder I try to ignore it, the louder it gets, until I just have to stop what I'm doing. It's just exhausting having to fight my own brain like that, you know? It just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or rituals in response to these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, that's... that's really what I was getting at when I first came in and was telling you about losing all those massive chunks of time. It's exactly that. When those thoughts hit me, I can't just sit there and feel anxious about it, I actually feel like I have to do something to fix it, or at least try to prove to myself that the thought isn't true. \n\nSo, like I mentioned earlier with the emails and the texts—if my brain says, \"Hey, you sent the wrong attachment to that client,\" I can't just brush it off. I have to go into my email client, go to the sent folder, find the exact email, and open it. And I'll read it. Every single word. I'll look at the file name of the attachment. And I'll say to myself, \"Okay, see? It's the right file.\" And I'll close it. \n\nBut then, maybe ten seconds later, the thought is back, and it's whispering, \"Are you sure? Maybe you misread the file extension. Maybe you missed a typo.\" So I have to open the folder again. I'll open and close the same directory on my desktop over and over and over, checking the files, closing it, opening it back up. It’s like I’m trapped in this loop. And it's not a quick thing, either. I'll spend forty-five minutes just clicking the same folder, reading the same text message, trying to get this... this sense of certainty that just never seems to stick. \n\nAnd it's the exact same process with the mental stuff I was talking about, you know, when I'm stuck by the front door before I leave for work. I'll be holding my keys, ready to walk out, and a thought about a conversation from yesterday pops up. I have to stand right there and mentally replay the entire interaction from start to finish. If I get distracted halfway through the memory, or if I can't remember the exact tone of voice the other person used, I feel this intense pressure to start the whole replay over from the very beginning. I'm just standing there in the hallway, completely frozen, going through this mental script again and again, just desperately trying to prove to myself I didn't make some serious mistake or offend someone. I literally cannot turn the doorknob and leave the house, or sit down at my desk and actually start my IT work, until I've done these routines. That's why the time just vanishes. I just... I have to do it, even though I know how completely irrational it is while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have these rituals and repetitive behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it really has. I mean, it’s hard to hide it when you’re losing that much time, you know? At work, especially with my coworkers, it’s becoming a real problem. I’ve always prided myself on being the reliable one—the guy who gets the system specs done on time, the one people can count on. But because my work has slowed down so much with all this... this checking and re-reading and getting stuck in my head, I’ve actually missed a couple of major design milestones recently. The project managers are starting to ask questions, and I don't know what to tell them. I can't exactly say, \"Sorry the architecture document is late, I spent three hours opening and closing a desktop folder.\" \n\nAnd it’s not just the technical deliverables, either. I usually handle a lot of the mentoring for the new hires. I really enjoy doing it, sitting down with them and walking them through our legacy systems. But lately, I’ve had to postpone those mentoring sessions constantly. I'll look at the clock, realize I've lost an hour checking my outbox, and I have to message them and push the meeting back. I used to handle all of that so reliably, and now I'm just constantly making excuses to these young kids who are looking to me for guidance.\n\nAnd then... well, it’s bleeding into my personal life and community stuff, too, which really hurts. There’s this youth coding workshop that I volunteer for. It really, really matters to me—giving back to the kids, showing them the ropes, getting them excited about computers. But the other weekend, I got stuck at my front door doing that exact mental replay thing I was telling you about earlier. I was just standing there with my keys, agonizing over some passing comment I made to the coordinator the day before, just playing it over and over. By the time I finally convinced myself I hadn't offended him and managed to actually leave the house, I was incredibly late. I walked in totally flustered, completely unprepared for the lesson, and the other volunteers had had to stall for me. I was just... I was so profoundly embarrassed. I felt like I let everyone down—the kids, my friends running the program... It’s just making me feel so awful because I can't really explain to any of them why I'm suddenly acting so flaky and undependable.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F42"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

But lately, I’ve had to postpone those mentoring sessions constantly. I'll look at the clock, realize I've lost an hour checking my outbox, and I have to message them and push the meeting back. I used to handle all of that so reliably, and now I'm just constantly making excuses to these young kids who are looking to me for guidance.\n\nAnd then... well, it’s bleeding into my personal life and community stuff, too, which really hurts. There’s this youth coding workshop that I volunteer for. It really, really matters to me—giving back to the kids, showing them the ropes, getting them excited about computers. But the other weekend, I got stuck at my front door doing that exact mental replay thing I was telling you about earlier. I was just standing there with my keys, agonizing over some passing comment I made to the coordinator the day before, just playing it over and over. By the time I finally convinced myself I hadn't offended him and managed to actually leave the house, I was incredibly late. I walked in totally flustered, completely unprepared for the lesson, and the other volunteers had had to stall for me. I was just... I was so profoundly embarrassed. I felt like I let everyone down—the kids, my friends running the program... It’s just making me feel so awful because I can't really explain to any of them why I'm suddenly acting so flaky and undependable.\n\n[Last Question]\nHow would you describe the level of distress these experiences are causing you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 56 / computer_occupation / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Distress... gosh, I mean, it’s... it’s just this constant, heavy weight. If I had to really describe the feeling, it’s just so incredibly draining. You know, in my line of work, when you’re building out these large-scale network architectures or trying to untangle some messy legacy systems all day, your brain naturally gets tired. You expect that. But this... this is a completely different kind of exhaustion. It’s like... well, it’s like having a rogue background process on a server that’s just eating up 99% of the CPU, and you can’t force-quit it no matter what you do, and eventually the whole machine just grinds to a halt. That’s exactly what my brain feels like every single day. 

And the emotional toll of that, the sheer frustration of it... it’s just huge. I’m 56 years old, I have my degree, I’ve built a really solid career, and like I said, I’ve always been the dependable one. The guy people look up to. And to suddenly feel like I have zero control over my own mind... it makes me feel so profoundly ashamed. I’ll just be sitting there at my desk, practically in tears sometimes, just feeling so completely overwhelmed and, frankly, stupid, because I know—I logically *know*—that checking my outbox for the fiftieth time makes absolutely no sense. 

But the anxiety just builds and builds in my chest, this tight, awful feeling, until I feel like I'm going to jump out of my skin if I don't just give in and do the check. I don't know how you'd measure it on a scale or anything, because it's not like I'm completely bedridden, you know? I still manage to get myself to the office, I still put my clothes on and try to live my life, but the distress is just... it's always there. It's this loud, exhausting hum in the background that just sucks the joy out of everything I used to love doing.

I came in because I keep losing these massive chunks of time, and it's incredibly frustrating. I work with computers—I've been doing IT and systems work ever since I got my bachelor's—so I'm at a screen all day, and lately, I just can't seem to actually start my work. I'll sit down at my desk, and I find myself endlessly re-reading my emails and texts. I'll read an email I sent the day before, scrutinizing every single word, and then I'll open a folder on my desktop, look at the files, close it, and then I just have to reopen it. I'll open and close the same directory over and over again. \n\nAnd it’s not even just when I'm at the computer, either. It happens before I even leave the house in the morning. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself that I didn’t make some serious mistake. I get this horrible nagging feeling that I said something awful, or that I accidentally deleted a crucial file, or sent the wrong attachment to a client, and I just have to keep checking my outbox or my memory to prove that I didn't ruin everything. By the time I finally convince myself it's okay and actually leave the house or start my tasks, an hour or two has just vanished, and I'm already exhausted before the day has even really begun. I just... I can't seem to let things be without checking them a dozen times.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having persistent, intrusive thoughts that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, all the time. It’ll be this sudden, intense fear that I might have sent the wrong message to my ex-wife—something completely inappropriate or that could be taken the wrong way. Or I'll get it in my head that I somehow mishandled some old family photos I was backing up, like I accidentally wiped the drive and lost them forever. Or sometimes it's this heavy, sinking feeling that I've let someone in the community down, like I made some careless mistake that really hurt somebody. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is, I know I've already been over it. I tell myself, \"You already thought about this yesterday, it's fine, you didn't do anything wrong, just get back to work.\" I try so, so hard to just push it away and ignore it. It's exactly that. When those thoughts hit me, I can't just sit there and feel anxious about it, I actually feel like I have to do something to fix it, or at least try to prove to myself that the thought isn't true. \n\nSo, like I mentioned earlier with the emails and the texts—if my brain says, \"Hey, you sent the wrong attachment to that client,\" I can't just brush it off. I have to go into my email client, go to the sent folder, find the exact email, and open it. And I'll read it. Every single word. I'll look at the file name of the attachment. When those thoughts hit me, I can't just sit there and feel anxious about it, I actually feel like I have to do something to fix it, or at least try to prove to myself that the thought isn't true. \n\nSo, like I mentioned earlier with the emails and the texts—if my brain says, \"Hey, you sent the wrong attachment to that client,\" I can't just brush it off. I have to go into my email client, go to the sent folder, find the exact email, and open it. And I'll read it. Every single word. I'll look at the file name of the attachment. And I'll say to myself, \"Okay, see? It's the right file.\" And I'll close it. \n\nBut then, maybe ten seconds later, the thought is back, and it's whispering, \"Are you sure? Maybe you misread the file extension. Maybe you missed a typo.\" So I have to open the folder again. I'll open and close the same directory on my desktop over and over and over, checking the files, closing it, opening it back up. It’s like I’m trapped in this loop. And it's not a quick thing, either. I'll spend forty-five minutes just clicking the same folder, reading the same text message, trying to get this... this sense of certainty that just never seems to stick. \n\nAnd it's the exact same process with the mental stuff I was talking about, you know, when I'm stuck by the front door before I leave for work. I'll be holding my keys, ready to walk out, and a thought about a conversation from yesterday pops up. I have to stand right there and mentally replay the entire interaction from start to finish. If I get distracted halfway through the memory, or if I can't remember the exact tone of voice the other person used, I feel this intense pressure to start the whole replay over from the very beginning. I'm just standing there in the hallway, completely frozen, going through this mental script again and again, just desperately trying to prove to myself I didn't make some serious mistake or offend someone. I literally cannot turn the doorknob and leave the house, or sit down at my desk and actually start my IT work, until I've done these routines. That's why the time just vanishes. I just... I have to do it, even though I know how completely irrational it is while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have these rituals and repetitive behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it really has. I mean, it’s hard to hide it when you’re losing that much time, you know? At work, especially with my coworkers, it’s becoming a real problem. I’ve always prided myself on being the reliable one—the guy who gets the system specs done on time, the one people can count on. But because my work has slowed down so much with all this... this checking and re-reading and getting stuck in my head, I’ve actually missed a couple of major design milestones recently. The project managers are starting to ask questions, and I don't know what to tell them. I can't exactly say, \"Sorry the architecture document is late, I spent three hours opening and closing a desktop folder.\" \n\nAnd it’s not just the technical deliverables, either. I usually handle a lot of the mentoring for the new hires. I really enjoy doing it, sitting down with them and walking them through our legacy systems. But lately, I’ve had to postpone those mentoring sessions constantly. I'll look at the clock, realize I've lost an hour checking my outbox, and I have to message them and push the meeting back. I used to handle all of that so reliably, and now I'm just constantly making excuses to these young kids who are looking to me for guidance.\n\nAnd then... well, it’s bleeding into my personal life and community stuff, too, which really hurts. There’s this youth coding workshop that I volunteer for. It really, really matters to me—giving back to the kids, showing them the ropes, getting them excited about computers. But the other weekend, I got stuck at my front door doing that exact mental replay thing I was telling you about earlier. I was just standing there with my keys, agonizing over some passing comment I made to the coordinator the day before, just playing it over and over. By the time I finally convinced myself I hadn't offended him and managed to actually leave the house, I was incredibly late. I walked in totally flustered, completely unprepared for the lesson, and the other volunteers had had to stall for me. I was just... I was so profoundly embarrassed. I felt like I let everyone down—the kids, my friends running the program... It’s just making me feel so awful because I can't really explain to any of them why I'm suddenly acting so flaky and undependable.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress these experiences are causing you?\n\nPatient: Distress... gosh, I mean, it’s... it’s just this constant, heavy weight. If I had to really describe the feeling, it’s just so incredibly draining. You know, in my line of work, when you’re building out these large-scale network architectures or trying to untangle some messy legacy systems all day, your brain naturally gets tired. You expect that. But this... this is a completely different kind of exhaustion. It’s like... well, it’s like having a rogue background process on a server that’s just eating up 99% of the CPU, and you can’t force-quit it no matter what you do, and eventually the whole machine just grinds to a halt. That’s exactly what my brain feels like every single day. \n\nAnd the emotional toll of that, the sheer frustration of it... it’s just huge. I’m 56 years old, I have my degree, I’ve built a really solid career, and like I said, I’ve always been the dependable one. The guy people look up to. And to suddenly feel like I have zero control over my own mind... it makes me feel so profoundly ashamed. I’ll just be sitting there at my desk, practically in tears sometimes, just feeling so completely overwhelmed and, frankly, stupid, because I know—I logically *know*—that checking my outbox for the fiftieth time makes absolutely no sense. \n\nBut the anxiety just builds and builds in my chest, this tight, awful feeling, until I feel like I'm going to jump out of my skin if I don't just give in and do the check. I don't know how you'd measure it on a scale or anything, because it's not like I'm completely bedridden, you know? I still manage to get myself to the office, I still put my clothes on and try to live my life, but the distress is just... it's always there. It's this loud, exhausting hum in the background that just sucks the joy out of everything I used to love doing.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre you having difficulty maintaining relationships or fulfilling responsibilities due to these experiences?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 6\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

Well, yeah, I mean... I think that really goes back to what I was just saying a few minutes ago, right? It’s absolutely impacting my responsibilities, across the board. 

Like I was telling you with my job... my actual day-to-day output has slowed down so much that I’ve outright missed those major design milestones. I’ve had to postpone them over and over because I'm just... stuck. I'll be stuck at my desk, losing hours to checking my outbox, and I have to send them a message making up some excuse about why we can't meet. 

And it’s the exact same story with my personal commitments, like that youth coding workshop I mentioned earlier. That’s a responsibility I take really, really seriously. I’ve always been the guy who shows up early to set up the hardware, get the programs running for the kids... and instead, because I couldn't get through my front door without replaying that memory a dozen times, I ended up walking in late and completely unprepared. I was just so profoundly embarrassed, you know? I had to have the other volunteers cover for me. 

So, yes, to answer your question... maintaining these responsibilities, whether it's at the office or out in my community, it's becoming incredibly difficult. It’s like I’m just standing by watching myself drop the ball on all these things that I care deeply about, and no matter how hard I try, I just can't seem to stop it from happening.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Avoidance... well, no. No, I don't think so. I mean, it would almost be easier if I did, wouldn't it? If I just completely avoided the things that trigger all this... this mental gridlock. But honestly, no, I haven't been avoiding any activities or situations. It’s actually quite the opposite, which is partly why it’s so incredibly exhausting. 

I still force myself to do everything I normally do. I still get up, get dressed, drive to the office, sit down at my desk, and log into the network. I don't avoid my email client, even though I know—I know with absolute certainty—that the second I open my outbox, I'm probably going to lose an hour checking to see if my messages actually sent correctly. I still open the architecture documents. I still schedule the mentoring sessions with the junior developers, even if I end up having to push them back later. 

And like I was saying about the youth coding workshop... if I were avoiding things, I could have just texted the coordinator and said, "Hey, I'm under the weather, I can't make it today." That would have spared me all that profound embarrassment of walking in late. But I didn't do that. I still tried to go. I just ended up trapped at my own front door, agonizing over my thoughts for God knows how long. So, I'm not steering clear of the front door, and I'm not staying away from the community center, and I'm certainly not hiding from my computer at work. 

I'm still throwing myself into all these situations every single day, hoping that somehow, magically, my brain will just process things normally this time. I keep showing up for the exact same routines, the same responsibilities... I'm just getting completely bogged down once I'm actually in them. So no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding anything at all. I'm just... I'm just failing to get through them efficiently.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's really starting to take a toll on my day-to-day life, and I just don't know how to snap out of it. I came in because I keep losing these massive chunks of time, and it's incredibly frustrating. I work with computers—I've been doing IT and systems work ever since I got my bachelor's—so I'm at a screen all day, and lately, I just can't seem to actually start my work. I'll sit down at my desk, and I find myself endlessly re-reading my emails and texts. I'll read an email I sent the day before, scrutinizing every single word, and then I'll open a folder on my desktop, look at the files, close it, and then I just have to reopen it. I'll open and close the same directory over and over again. \n\nAnd it’s not even just when I'm at the computer, either. It happens before I even leave the house in the morning. I'll be standing right by the front door, keys in my hand, ready to go, and I'll just freeze up because I start mentally replaying conversations I had from the day before. I'll play them over and over in my head, dissecting every sentence, trying to remember exactly what I said and how the other person reacted. I just get stuck there in the hallway. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself that I didn’t make some serious mistake. I get this horrible nagging feeling that I said something awful, or that I accidentally deleted a crucial file, or sent the wrong attachment to a client, and I just have to keep checking my outbox or my memory to prove that I didn't ruin everything. By the time I finally convince myself it's okay and actually leave the house or start my tasks, an hour or two has just vanished, and I'm already exhausted before the day has even really begun. I just... I can't seem to let things be without checking them a dozen times.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having persistent, intrusive thoughts that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, all the time. It's... it's exactly like that. I'll be sitting there, right in the middle of trying to work through a complicated system design, or maybe I'm trying to prepare a lesson or a tutorial for some of the new hires, and I really need to concentrate. But then, out of nowhere, this thought just barges right in. \n\nIt doesn't even have to do with work most of the time! It’ll be this sudden, intense fear that I might have sent the wrong message to my ex-wife—something completely inappropriate or that could be taken the wrong way. Or I'll get it in my head that I somehow mishandled some old family photos I was backing up, like I accidentally wiped the drive and lost them forever. Or sometimes it's this heavy, sinking feeling that I've let someone in the community down, like I made some careless mistake that really hurt somebody. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is, I know I've already been over it. I tell myself, \"You already thought about this yesterday, it's fine, you didn't do anything wrong, just get back to work.\" I try so, so hard to just push it away and ignore it. I'll stare at my monitor and try to force my brain back to the system design, but I just... I can't seem to let it pass. It just sits there, nagging at me, completely hijacking my focus, even though I know, logically, that I've already dealt with it. I'll try to suppress it, but the harder I try to ignore it, the louder it gets, until I just have to stop what I'm doing. It's just exhausting having to fight my own brain like that, you know? It just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or rituals in response to these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, that's... that's really what I was getting at when I first came in and was telling you about losing all those massive chunks of time. It's exactly that. When those thoughts hit me, I can't just sit there and feel anxious about it, I actually feel like I have to do something to fix it, or at least try to prove to myself that the thought isn't true. \n\nSo, like I mentioned earlier with the emails and the texts—if my brain says, \"Hey, you sent the wrong attachment to that client,\" I can't just brush it off. I have to go into my email client, go to the sent folder, find the exact email, and open it. And I'll read it. Every single word. I'll look at the file name of the attachment. And I'll say to myself, \"Okay, see? It's the right file.\" And I'll close it. \n\nBut then, maybe ten seconds later, the thought is back, and it's whispering, \"Are you sure? Maybe you misread the file extension. Maybe you missed a typo.\" So I have to open the folder again. I'll open and close the same directory on my desktop over and over and over, checking the files, closing it, opening it back up. It’s like I’m trapped in this loop. And it's not a quick thing, either. I'll spend forty-five minutes just clicking the same folder, reading the same text message, trying to get this... this sense of certainty that just never seems to stick. \n\nAnd it's the exact same process with the mental stuff I was talking about, you know, when I'm stuck by the front door before I leave for work. I'll be holding my keys, ready to walk out, and a thought about a conversation from yesterday pops up. I have to stand right there and mentally replay the entire interaction from start to finish. If I get distracted halfway through the memory, or if I can't remember the exact tone of voice the other person used, I feel this intense pressure to start the whole replay over from the very beginning. I'm just standing there in the hallway, completely frozen, going through this mental script again and again, just desperately trying to prove to myself I didn't make some serious mistake or offend someone. I literally cannot turn the doorknob and leave the house, or sit down at my desk and actually start my IT work, until I've done these routines. That's why the time just vanishes. I just... I have to do it, even though I know how completely irrational it is while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have these rituals and repetitive behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it really has. I mean, it’s hard to hide it when you’re losing that much time, you know? At work, especially with my coworkers, it’s becoming a real problem. I’ve always prided myself on being the reliable one—the guy who gets the system specs done on time, the one people can count on. But because my work has slowed down so much with all this... this checking and re-reading and getting stuck in my head, I’ve actually missed a couple of major design milestones recently. The project managers are starting to ask questions, and I don't know what to tell them. I can't exactly say, \"Sorry the architecture document is late, I spent three hours opening and closing a desktop folder.\" \n\nAnd it’s not just the technical deliverables, either. I usually handle a lot of the mentoring for the new hires. I really enjoy doing it, sitting down with them and walking them through our legacy systems. But lately, I’ve had to postpone those mentoring sessions constantly. I'll look at the clock, realize I've lost an hour checking my outbox, and I have to message them and push the meeting back. I used to handle all of that so reliably, and now I'm just constantly making excuses to these young kids who are looking to me for guidance.\n\nAnd then... well, it’s bleeding into my personal life and community stuff, too, which really hurts. There’s this youth coding workshop that I volunteer for. It really, really matters to me—giving back to the kids, showing them the ropes, getting them excited about computers. But the other weekend, I got stuck at my front door doing that exact mental replay thing I was telling you about earlier. I was just standing there with my keys, agonizing over some passing comment I made to the coordinator the day before, just playing it over and over. By the time I finally convinced myself I hadn't offended him and managed to actually leave the house, I was incredibly late. I walked in totally flustered, completely unprepared for the lesson, and the other volunteers had had to stall for me. I was just... I was so profoundly embarrassed. I felt like I let everyone down—the kids, my friends running the program... It’s just making me feel so awful because I can't really explain to any of them why I'm suddenly acting so flaky and undependable.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress these experiences are causing you?\n\nPatient: Distress... gosh, I mean, it’s... it’s just this constant, heavy weight. If I had to really describe the feeling, it’s just so incredibly draining. You know, in my line of work, when you’re building out these large-scale network architectures or trying to untangle some messy legacy systems all day, your brain naturally gets tired. You expect that. But this... this is a completely different kind of exhaustion. It’s like... well, it’s like having a rogue background process on a server that’s just eating up 99% of the CPU, and you can’t force-quit it no matter what you do, and eventually the whole machine just grinds to a halt. That’s exactly what my brain feels like every single day. \n\nAnd the emotional toll of that, the sheer frustration of it... it’s just huge. I’m 56 years old, I have my degree, I’ve built a really solid career, and like I said, I’ve always been the dependable one. The guy people look up to. And to suddenly feel like I have zero control over my own mind... it makes me feel so profoundly ashamed. I’ll just be sitting there at my desk, practically in tears sometimes, just feeling so completely overwhelmed and, frankly, stupid, because I know—I logically *know*—that checking my outbox for the fiftieth time makes absolutely no sense. \n\nBut the anxiety just builds and builds in my chest, this tight, awful feeling, until I feel like I'm going to jump out of my skin if I don't just give in and do the check. I don't know how you'd measure it on a scale or anything, because it's not like I'm completely bedridden, you know? I still manage to get myself to the office, I still put my clothes on and try to live my life, but the distress is just... it's always there. It's this loud, exhausting hum in the background that just sucks the joy out of everything I used to love doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships or fulfilling responsibilities due to these experiences?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... I think that really goes back to what I was just saying a few minutes ago, right? It’s absolutely impacting my responsibilities, across the board. \n\nLike I was telling you with my job... my actual day-to-day output has slowed down so much that I’ve outright missed those major design milestones. And in network architecture, missing a milestone isn't just a tiny hiccup, it creates this massive bottleneck for everyone else down the pipeline who is waiting on my specs. It makes me look incredibly undependable. And then there are those mentoring sessions with the junior staff that I used to handle so reliably. I’ve had to postpone them over and over because I'm just... stuck. I'll be stuck at my desk, losing hours to checking my outbox, and I have to send them a message making up some excuse about why we can't meet. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same story with my personal commitments, like that youth coding workshop I mentioned earlier. That’s a responsibility I take really, really seriously. I’ve always been the guy who shows up early to set up the hardware, get the programs running for the kids... and instead, because I couldn't get through my front door without replaying that memory a dozen times, I ended up walking in late and completely unprepared. I was just so profoundly embarrassed, you know? I had to have the other volunteers cover for me. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question... maintaining these responsibilities, whether it's at the office or out in my community, it's becoming incredibly difficult. It’s like I’m just standing by watching myself drop the ball on all these things that I care deeply about, and no matter how hard I try, I just can't seem to stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or situations that you've been avoiding because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Avoidance... well, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Physical actions... yes, yes, I do. And honestly, that’s exactly why I finally made the appointment to come in and see you. It’s these actions, these... these repetitive loops I get stuck in. 

Like I was saying earlier about my outbox—it's not just a passing thought. I am physically clicking, opening, closing, and reopening the same folders over and over again. I'll send an email, a perfectly routine email to a client or a junior dev, and then I have to go into the sent folder, open the message, re-read it, close it, and then... I just have to do it again. And again. I'll sit there and re-read the exact same text message five, six, seven times, scrolling up and down, checking the timestamps, making absolutely sure I didn't type something inappropriate or completely ruin the system architecture specs. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself, beyond a shadow of a doubt, that I didn’t make some catastrophic mistake. 

And it’s not just on the computer, either. It’s physical actions before I even start my day. Before I can leave the house, or before I can even begin a new task at work, I find myself standing there, physically frozen, while I mentally replay entire conversations I had the day before. I'll stand by the front door, hand on the knob, just... staring into space, going over and over what I said to the project manager on Tuesday. Did I sound rude? Did I give the wrong IP range? Did I accidentally agree to a deadline we can't meet? I have to replay the memory perfectly, from start to finish, before I can physically turn the doorknob and walk outside. 

If I lose my place in the memory, or if it doesn't feel... I don't know, 'right' somehow... I have to start the whole mental replay over again. And the whole time, I'm just standing there, losing these massive chunks of time. Ten minutes, twenty minutes, half an hour, just... gone. Just completely eaten up by reopening folders and replaying conversations. It's incredibly physical, and it's just so, so draining.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it's... it's really starting to take a toll on my day-to-day life, and I just don't know how to snap out of it. I came in because I keep losing these massive chunks of time, and it's incredibly frustrating. I work with computers—I've been doing IT and systems work ever since I got my bachelor's—so I'm at a screen all day, and lately, I just can't seem to actually start my work. I'll sit down at my desk, and I find myself endlessly re-reading my emails and texts. I'll read an email I sent the day before, scrutinizing every single word, and then I'll open a folder on my desktop, look at the files, close it, and then I just have to reopen it. I'll open and close the same directory over and over again. \n\nAnd it’s not even just when I'm at the computer, either. It happens before I even leave the house in the morning. I'll be standing right by the front door, keys in my hand, ready to go, and I'll just freeze up because I start mentally replaying conversations I had from the day before. I'll play them over and over in my head, dissecting every sentence, trying to remember exactly what I said and how the other person reacted. I just get stuck there in the hallway. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself that I didn’t make some serious mistake. I get this horrible nagging feeling that I said something awful, or that I accidentally deleted a crucial file, or sent the wrong attachment to a client, and I just have to keep checking my outbox or my memory to prove that I didn't ruin everything. By the time I finally convince myself it's okay and actually leave the house or start my tasks, an hour or two has just vanished, and I'm already exhausted before the day has even really begun. I just... I can't seem to let things be without checking them a dozen times.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself having persistent, intrusive thoughts that you try to suppress or ignore?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, all the time. It's... it's exactly like that. I'll be sitting there, right in the middle of trying to work through a complicated system design, or maybe I'm trying to prepare a lesson or a tutorial for some of the new hires, and I really need to concentrate. But then, out of nowhere, this thought just barges right in. \n\nIt doesn't even have to do with work most of the time! It’ll be this sudden, intense fear that I might have sent the wrong message to my ex-wife—something completely inappropriate or that could be taken the wrong way. Or I'll get it in my head that I somehow mishandled some old family photos I was backing up, like I accidentally wiped the drive and lost them forever. Or sometimes it's this heavy, sinking feeling that I've let someone in the community down, like I made some careless mistake that really hurt somebody. \n\nAnd the frustrating part is, I know I've already been over it. I tell myself, \"You already thought about this yesterday, it's fine, you didn't do anything wrong, just get back to work.\" I try so, so hard to just push it away and ignore it. I'll stare at my monitor and try to force my brain back to the system design, but I just... I can't seem to let it pass. It just sits there, nagging at me, completely hijacking my focus, even though I know, logically, that I've already dealt with it. I'll try to suppress it, but the harder I try to ignore it, the louder it gets, until I just have to stop what I'm doing. It's just exhausting having to fight my own brain like that, you know? It just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or rituals in response to these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, that's... that's really what I was getting at when I first came in and was telling you about losing all those massive chunks of time. It's exactly that. When those thoughts hit me, I can't just sit there and feel anxious about it, I actually feel like I have to do something to fix it, or at least try to prove to myself that the thought isn't true. \n\nSo, like I mentioned earlier with the emails and the texts—if my brain says, \"Hey, you sent the wrong attachment to that client,\" I can't just brush it off. I have to go into my email client, go to the sent folder, find the exact email, and open it. And I'll read it. Every single word. I'll look at the file name of the attachment. And I'll say to myself, \"Okay, see? It's the right file.\" And I'll close it. \n\nBut then, maybe ten seconds later, the thought is back, and it's whispering, \"Are you sure? Maybe you misread the file extension. Maybe you missed a typo.\" So I have to open the folder again. I'll open and close the same directory on my desktop over and over and over, checking the files, closing it, opening it back up. It’s like I’m trapped in this loop. And it's not a quick thing, either. I'll spend forty-five minutes just clicking the same folder, reading the same text message, trying to get this... this sense of certainty that just never seems to stick. \n\nAnd it's the exact same process with the mental stuff I was talking about, you know, when I'm stuck by the front door before I leave for work. I'll be holding my keys, ready to walk out, and a thought about a conversation from yesterday pops up. I have to stand right there and mentally replay the entire interaction from start to finish. If I get distracted halfway through the memory, or if I can't remember the exact tone of voice the other person used, I feel this intense pressure to start the whole replay over from the very beginning. I'm just standing there in the hallway, completely frozen, going through this mental script again and again, just desperately trying to prove to myself I didn't make some serious mistake or offend someone. I literally cannot turn the doorknob and leave the house, or sit down at my desk and actually start my IT work, until I've done these routines. That's why the time just vanishes. I just... I have to do it, even though I know how completely irrational it is while I'm doing it.\n\nDoctor: Have these rituals and repetitive behaviors been affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah... yeah, it really has. I mean, it’s hard to hide it when you’re losing that much time, you know? At work, especially with my coworkers, it’s becoming a real problem. I’ve always prided myself on being the reliable one—the guy who gets the system specs done on time, the one people can count on. But because my work has slowed down so much with all this... this checking and re-reading and getting stuck in my head, I’ve actually missed a couple of major design milestones recently. The project managers are starting to ask questions, and I don't know what to tell them. I can't exactly say, \"Sorry the architecture document is late, I spent three hours opening and closing a desktop folder.\" \n\nAnd it’s not just the technical deliverables, either. I usually handle a lot of the mentoring for the new hires. I really enjoy doing it, sitting down with them and walking them through our legacy systems. But lately, I’ve had to postpone those mentoring sessions constantly. I'll look at the clock, realize I've lost an hour checking my outbox, and I have to message them and push the meeting back. I used to handle all of that so reliably, and now I'm just constantly making excuses to these young kids who are looking to me for guidance.\n\nAnd then... well, it’s bleeding into my personal life and community stuff, too, which really hurts. There’s this youth coding workshop that I volunteer for. It really, really matters to me—giving back to the kids, showing them the ropes, getting them excited about computers. But the other weekend, I got stuck at my front door doing that exact mental replay thing I was telling you about earlier. I was just standing there with my keys, agonizing over some passing comment I made to the coordinator the day before, just playing it over and over. By the time I finally convinced myself I hadn't offended him and managed to actually leave the house, I was incredibly late. I walked in totally flustered, completely unprepared for the lesson, and the other volunteers had had to stall for me. I was just... I was so profoundly embarrassed. I felt like I let everyone down—the kids, my friends running the program... It’s just making me feel so awful because I can't really explain to any of them why I'm suddenly acting so flaky and undependable.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress these experiences are causing you?\n\nPatient: Distress... gosh, I mean, it’s... it’s just this constant, heavy weight. If I had to really describe the feeling, it’s just so incredibly draining. You know, in my line of work, when you’re building out these large-scale network architectures or trying to untangle some messy legacy systems all day, your brain naturally gets tired. You expect that. But this... this is a completely different kind of exhaustion. It’s like... well, it’s like having a rogue background process on a server that’s just eating up 99% of the CPU, and you can’t force-quit it no matter what you do, and eventually the whole machine just grinds to a halt. That’s exactly what my brain feels like every single day. \n\nAnd the emotional toll of that, the sheer frustration of it... it’s just huge. I’m 56 years old, I have my degree, I’ve built a really solid career, and like I said, I’ve always been the dependable one. The guy people look up to. And to suddenly feel like I have zero control over my own mind... it makes me feel so profoundly ashamed. I’ll just be sitting there at my desk, practically in tears sometimes, just feeling so completely overwhelmed and, frankly, stupid, because I know—I logically *know*—that checking my outbox for the fiftieth time makes absolutely no sense. \n\nBut the anxiety just builds and builds in my chest, this tight, awful feeling, until I feel like I'm going to jump out of my skin if I don't just give in and do the check. I don't know how you'd measure it on a scale or anything, because it's not like I'm completely bedridden, you know? I still manage to get myself to the office, I still put my clothes on and try to live my life, but the distress is just... it's always there. It's this loud, exhausting hum in the background that just sucks the joy out of everything I used to love doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you having difficulty maintaining relationships or fulfilling responsibilities due to these experiences?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, I mean... I think that really goes back to what I was just saying a few minutes ago, right? It’s absolutely impacting my responsibilities, across the board. \n\nLike I was telling you with my job... my actual day-to-day output has slowed down so much that I’ve outright missed those major design milestones. And in network architecture, missing a milestone isn't just a tiny hiccup, it creates this massive bottleneck for everyone else down the pipeline who is waiting on my specs. It makes me look incredibly undependable. And then there are those mentoring sessions with the junior staff that I used to handle so reliably. I’ve had to postpone them over and over because I'm just... stuck. I'll be stuck at my desk, losing hours to checking my outbox, and I have to send them a message making up some excuse about why we can't meet. \n\nAnd it’s the exact same story with my personal commitments, like that youth coding workshop I mentioned earlier. That’s a responsibility I take really, really seriously. I’ve always been the guy who shows up early to set up the hardware, get the programs running for the kids... and instead, because I couldn't get through my front door without replaying that memory a dozen times, I ended up walking in late and completely unprepared. I was just so profoundly embarrassed, you know? I had to have the other volunteers cover for me. \n\nSo, yes, to answer your question... maintaining these responsibilities, whether it's at the office or out in my community, it's becoming incredibly difficult. It’s like I’m just standing by watching myself drop the ball on all these things that I care deeply about, and no matter how hard I try, I just can't seem to stop it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities or situations that you've been avoiding because of these intrusive thoughts or rituals?\n\nPatient: Avoidance... well, no. No, I don't think so. I mean, it would almost be easier if I did, wouldn't it? If I just completely avoided the things that trigger all this... this mental gridlock. But honestly, no, I haven't been avoiding any activities or situations. It’s actually quite the opposite, which is partly why it’s so incredibly exhausting. \n\nI still force myself to do everything I normally do. I still get up, get dressed, drive to the office, sit down at my desk, and log into the network. I don't avoid my email client, even though I know—I know with absolute certainty—that the second I open my outbox, I'm probably going to lose an hour checking to see if my messages actually sent correctly. I still open the architecture documents. I still schedule the mentoring sessions with the junior developers, even if I end up having to push them back later. \n\nAnd like I was saying about the youth coding workshop... if I were avoiding things, I could have just texted the coordinator and said, \"Hey, I'm under the weather, I can't make it today.\" That would have spared me all that profound embarrassment of walking in late. So, I'm not steering clear of the front door, and I'm not staying away from the community center, and I'm certainly not hiding from my computer at work. \n\nI'm still throwing myself into all these situations every single day, hoping that somehow, magically, my brain will just process things normally this time. I keep showing up for the exact same routines, the same responsibilities... I'm just getting completely bogged down once I'm actually in them. So no, I wouldn't say I'm avoiding anything at all. I'm just... I'm just failing to get through them efficiently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical actions in response to these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Physical actions... yes, yes, I do. I am physically clicking, opening, closing, and reopening the same folders over and over again. I'll send an email, a perfectly routine email to a client or a junior dev, and then I have to go into the sent folder, open the message, re-read it, close it, and then... I just have to do it again. And again. I'll sit there and re-read the exact same text message five, six, seven times, scrolling up and down, checking the timestamps, making absolutely sure I didn't type something inappropriate or completely ruin the system architecture specs. It’s like I’m trying to prove to myself, beyond a shadow of a doubt, that I didn’t make some catastrophic mistake. \n\nAnd it’s not just on the computer, either. It’s physical actions before I even start my day. Arranging things... you mean, like, lining up the pens on my desk, or making sure my dual monitors are perfectly symmetrical? No, no, I don't really have any issues with that. 

It’s actually kind of funny you ask that, because in my line of work—you know, being a network architect—people just automatically assume I’m this hyper-organized, meticulous guy when it comes to my physical environment. They think I must have all my server racks perfectly color-coded and my desk completely pristine, with everything at exact ninety-degree angles. But honestly... if you were to walk into my office right now, or even look at my workspace in the den at home, it’s a complete mess. It really is. I’ve got coffee cups from three days ago, loose printouts of routing tables scattered everywhere, sticky notes overlapping all over the bezel of my screen... 

So, no, my brain just doesn't latch onto where the stapler is sitting or if a picture frame is crooked. I can walk right past a messy bookshelf and it doesn't bother me in the slightest. It doesn't give me that... that agonizing, paralyzing grip like the emails do. For me, it’s all about the content, you know? The fear of having made a catastrophic error in communication or in the system specs. The physical arrangement of objects in the room just doesn't trigger that same alarm bell in my head. 

I don't feel any urge whatsoever to straighten things out or put them in a specific order. The only thing I'm hyper-focused on at that desk is my email client, just sitting there opening and closing the sent folder like I was telling you before. But the desk itself? The objects? No, symmetry isn't something I worry about at all.

It just sits there, nagging at me, completely hijacking my focus, even though I know, logically, that I've already dealt with it. It just won't let up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to repeat certain actions or rituals in response to these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, that's... that's really what I was getting at when I first came in and was telling you about losing all those massive chunks of time. When those thoughts hit me, I can't just sit there and feel anxious about it, I actually feel like I have to do something to fix it, or at least try to prove to myself that the thought isn't true. \n\nSo, like I mentioned earlier with the emails and the texts—if my brain says, \"Hey, you sent the wrong attachment to that client,\" I can't just brush it off. I have to go into my email client, go to the sent folder, find the exact email, and open it. And I'll read it. Every single word. I'll look at the file name of the attachment. I'm just standing there in the hallway, completely frozen, going through this mental script again and again, just desperately trying to prove to myself I didn't make some serious mistake or offend someone. I'm just failing to get through them efficiently.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform any specific physical actions in response to these intrusive thoughts?\n\nPatient: Physical actions... yes, yes, I do. Did I accidentally agree to a deadline we can't meet? I don't know, 'right' somehow... I have to start the whole mental replay over again. And the whole time, I'm just standing there, losing these massive chunks of time. Ten minutes, twenty minutes, half an hour, just... gone. Just completely eaten up by reopening folders and replaying conversations. It's incredibly physical, and it's just so, so draining.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a strong need to arrange things in a specific order or symmetry?\n\nPatient: Arranging things... you mean, like, lining up the pens on my desk, or making sure my dual monitors are perfectly symmetrical? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Prevent something bad... yeah. Yes, exactly. I mean, like I was saying just a few minutes ago, that is the entire reason I finally picked up the phone and made this appointment. It’s... it's like I’m constantly on trial in my own head, and I have to frantically gather the evidence to prove my innocence. 

I just keep losing these huge chunks of time—just hours of my day completely slipping away—because I'm sitting there re-reading emails and texts, reopening those same folders on the network, or just standing frozen at the front door mentally replaying conversations before I can leave the house or even start my actual work. And it all comes back to exactly what you just asked. I’m doing all of this because I feel like I have to prove to myself—beyond any shadow of a doubt—that I didn’t make some serious mistake. 

Because, you know, in my line of work, a serious mistake isn't just a simple typo. If I configure a subnet wrong or send a client the wrong architecture specs, it could take down a whole system. It could cost thousands of dollars. So my brain just starts screaming, 'Did you write that right? Are you sure? You better check.' And then I check. But checking once isn't enough, because what if my eyes skipped over a crucial error when I checked it? What if I misremembered what I just read? So I have to reopen the folder. Re-read the text. Replay the entire conversation I had with the project manager to make absolutely sure I didn't verbally agree to a catastrophic rollout schedule. 

I'm just trying to prevent a disaster. I feel like if I don't do these... these loops, if I don't check everything a dozen times, something terrible is going to happen and it's going to be entirely my fault because I wasn't careful enough. It's exhausting, it really is, but I can't stop because the alternative—the feeling that I've ruined everything and just don't know it yet—is so much worse. So yeah, I'm losing all this time just trying to prove to myself that everything is actually okay.
